## Spelldancer

Spelldancers, valantra, or spellsingers, were a unique kind of mage in the Realms who relied on a form of magic based on song and dance.

Description
Spelldancers were known to outsiders in Waterdeep and the Dalelands as spellsingers, but that name had fallen out of favor by . They called themselves "valantra". Often people of wild and vibrant feelings, female spellsingers outnumbered men ten to one or more.

Skills
The spellsinger practice required great vigor, insight, and brainpower, and thus, all valantra were equally exceptional in those aspects, and inescapably, they had practice in both song and dance. None of them was familiar with the use of armor or any kind of shield.

To cast spells, valantra began a frenzied dance while entering a trance state, in which they opened their mind to phenomenal cosmic energies; this obviated any need for material components, but made their practice more difficult when casting spells lacking other kinds of components. Any spell that a wizard could cast was accessible to the valantra. After a time directly proportional to the spell's exclusiveness and power requirements, they had a chance, based on the quality of their dance, of achieving the desired result; depending on the power they attempted to tap, they also required about that many minutes of dance. Though success was far from assured for any spell, valantra had a significant chance to summon up spells of power that equivalent wizards, or even far greater ones, had not a chance of accessing on their own power. However, later sources explained that valantra were not capable of casting powerful spells alone. Other sources spoke of valantra preparing a spell through a long song and dance, releasing it later through an instant's playing on an instrument.

A valantra could dance longer to mitigate the difficulty of greater spells, but this dance was so extenuating that it couldn't be danced for long, with a limit of about eighteen minutes for even the heartiest of valantra. After the dance was done, a valantra needed about as long to rest. Valantra of some progress had the ability to instead use their dance for evasive purposes, but the lack of a magical trance halved the duration of their dance. Valantra of greater power could also seek to enthrall all viewers with their song and dance; such performances involved a song, which if sung in a language the targets understood, became far more likely to succeed- albeit it was far weaker against targets that misliked the valantra.

Ever more advanced valantra became ever more evasive in a focused dance, and their period of enthrallment grew even longer. At the pinnacle of the valantra spellsinger's art, they could use their dance to make onlookers fall asleep for at least an hour and forty minutes, perhaps twice that for those who honed their abilities to the extreme. Weaker onlookers were affected first, while those of superior prowess became targets only as the dance progressed.

Valantra could dance together in groups of three to eight in order to improve their odds of achieving their desired goals through the dance, but such circles were about as effective as the least of the dancing valantra. Thus, spellsingers only ever used such dances in groups of about level skill. When a valantra's song and dance fell short of the goal, the results could range from no effect to absolute disaster; the true nature of the disaster was unpredictable, but it was believed to be related to the effect sought, perhaps inverting targets, and perhaps taking other forms.

However, the exclusive dedication that the path of the valantra required of its practitioners meant that no valantra could ever learn different paths to power. Furthermore, it was incompatible with the creation of magical items, and valantra could neither use nor devise even spell scrolls. Valantra dance was an inborn talent, though it required refining.

Another known ability of spellsingers was spellsinger's luck, which was bestowed upon a few individuals in history. Spellsingers were also known to have emoployed a number of greater abilities singly such as the aforemetnioned luck, but they had been recorded to pay for such power with their lives. Another such power that had been manifested by a spelldancer was a spell that created wild magic throughout a large area, again, at the cost of the user's life.

By, they were instead referred to as spelldancers, both because the dance was at the heart of their power, and because it was too easy to mix them up with spellslingers, a term referring to about any mage. Furthermore, the spelldancers best known by that tiy had also me period used the dance to augment spells which they cast through more traditional means instead, only using their dance to apply metamagic to their spells. They also employed a sorcerous dance that undid the wits of their audience, acting akin to confusion, learned only after the dance of sleep. While their defensive dance was ineffective against most blows, it was recognized as being capable of avoiding all damage from many area spells.

History
In, Olothontor was fought off by three spellsingers after sinking a ship.While there is no indication it might be one kind of spellsinger over the other made explicit in the text, FOR9, Wizards and Rogues of the Realms claims that valantra spellsingers are a long-running mystery of the Realms and were included at the behest of Ed Greenwood. Taking such an assertion at face value, it would follow that older mentions of spellsingers by Ed Greenwood would refer to such spellsingers, rather than elven spellsingers such as the ones apparently first introduced in Elfsong. Thus, a number of such spellsinger mentions are explicitly rejected as valantra. Andarra's mention, being a part of Greenwood's opus, is assumed to refer to a valantra, while Alyniria's is concluded to be one, given it predates both Elfsong and any eponymous kits as well as being a part of Greenwood's opus. Olothonthor, due to the explicit mention of powers combining, is also assumed to have faced valantra spellsingers; the mention in the hill of lost souls, too, predates all other interpretations and is thus believed to be valantra.

During the Time of Troubles, Belkram Hardeth related that the spellsinger Andarra had spent the last of her lifeforce rendering magic wild in a battle against the Zhentarim in Greycloak Hills at some point in the past.

Mintiper Moonsilver received a dying brand from Alyniria, who used the last of her life to weave a lasting protection on him, and taught him the ancient Runetongue.

The Hill of Lost Souls was used as a secret meeting place by spellsingers as of the mid-1360's DR. As of, spellsingers were known to comprise part of the clergy of Finder Wyvernspur who counted two such as part of his clergy in  and Shiallia. The Harpers passed down stories of such spellcasters, but by the late mid-14 century, no Harper had any such ability.

Notable Valantra
The Dancing Witch of the Vilhon Reach
"Mad Athla" of Tashluta
The Singing Witch of the North
Andarra
Alyniria

Trivia
While the word "Valantra" referred actually to a nomadic group of the Heartlands, practitioners of this kind of spellsingers always called themselves "Valantra".

Spellsingers of Eilistraee were not the same as Valantra spellsingers.

## Spidersilk

Spidersilk or spider silk was a fiber produced by various species of spiders, as well as some spider-like creatures such as aranea, driders, and chitines. The term also referred to the processed form of the fiber, which was used for various textile purposes in the Realms.

Description
In its refined state, spidersilk lost the inherit stickiness found on spider threads. Armor and clothing fashioned from it tended to be rather quiet and lightweight.

Availability
 Some in the land of Chult manufactured spider silk from the local spiders and would refine their threads further with the aid of potash.
 In the Hordelands great amounts of spidersilk was found in Khopet-Dag, owing to the many giant spiders that lived in the mountain range.
 In the Duthcloim district of Menzoberranzan, Sh'aun Darnruel sold exquisite gowns of the finest spidersilk to the matron mothers.

History
In the, the House of Al-Kamari of Huzuz was experimenting with spider silk, even though the yield was low and the overhead was high. Mu'izzi al-Kamar gifted a vest and sash of spider silk cloth to Khalil al-Assad al-Zahir, while his sibling Dakim ibn Mu'izzi was working on a bedspread for him.

In the, drow used a hot-air balloon made from spider silk in the shape of a massive arachnid as they evacuated from Ithimir Isle off the coast of Akanûl. Webs from the balloon carried a cocoon as big as a small house, in which was transported an arambarium mother lode. Alongside it, hundreds of spiders and giant spiders carried ettercaps and undead miners and deployed webs that were caught by the winds and pulled them into the air to follow the balloon. Their destination was a portal to the Demonweb Pits.

Usages
 Fine carpets were sometimes made of spidersilk by master craftsmen in the Underdark.
 Chemises made of spidersilk were common nightwear among female nobility in the Blade Kingdoms.
 Cloaks were a common clothing item for spidersilk use, including the magical drow cloaks known as piwafwi.
 Spidersilk refined from the threads of giant spiders were commonly used by drow for bowstrings.
 Spidersilk was commonly used by drow as tripwires for traps.
 Robes.
 Rope could be made with spidersilk that was was exceptionally strong and lightweight. Such a practice was done by both some surface dwellers and drow.
 Spidersilk armor, a common form of armor in the Underdark, was typically made with specially treated spidersilk of aranea or driders.
 Spidersilk jerkin, a mysterious variety of light armor worn by drow kinslayers.
 Tunics.
 Paper.

Spell Components
 Some Halruaan spells required the silk of Halruaan glass spiders as a material component.
 The silk of a phase spider was a material component for the spell wardmist.
 The silk of a red spider was needed as material component for the spells nerve dance and stonefire.
 Older versions of the Banite spell stone walk required a piece of spidersilk that was at least a  in length in order to cast.
 A small ball of spidersilk was needed to cast the spell viscid glob.
 A simple strand of spidersilk was needed as a material component for the spells crown meld, Merald's meld, Oswald's mending, streamers, and zone of sweet air. The spell wondrous web in particular required six whole strands of spidersilk.

Notable Users
Individuals
 The drow goddess Lolth favored the guise of a lithe drow woman dressed in a gossamer gown woven from spider silk.
 Dreydre Freth owned a magical set of chainmail armor made from spidersilk, known as drow spidersilk armor.
 Kyleena Moonglow, a wild elf chieftain in the Delimbiyr Vale, wore silken armor made from polished treebark and Chultan spidersilk.
 Neheedra Duskryn owned five dresses made of spidersilk that had been shaped by drow magic.
Races & Sentient Creatures
 Chitines typically dressed in loose robes and tunics made of their own silk.
 Nixies were said to utilize the silk of giant water spiders.
Regions & Settlements
 The drow city of Menzoberranzan had numerous bridges made of hardened spidersilk connecting its many stone structures.

Religions
 The Church of Tiamat's clergy were known to wear diaphanous, multi-hued cloaks of spidersilk evocative of wings for ceremonies.
 The Shadoweirs, a Mielikkian order in the High Forest, wore for ceremonies cloaks that were woven by dryads from spidersilk and dyed green.

Other
 Spider rods could shoot strands of spidersilk out at targets from up to  away.

Spidersilk jerkins were in reality made using drider silk, rather than spidersilk from a regular giant spider. These armors were rare because of the dangers of procurement of the material, but the resulting light armor was as protective as masterwork studded leather armor while it weighed almost nothing and showed no restrictions to the wearer's movements. ## Spider Plagues

The Spider Plagues referred to a devastating disease outbreak that struck ancient Calimshan in the .

Effects
Victims of the Spider Plagues developed sores on their bodies which were filled with spider eggs. When these eggs hatched, they consumed the infected individual, killing them.

History
The Plagues impacted all cities in Calimshan and its territories in the surrounding region. The plague wiped out 32% of the general population, and 41% of Calimport.

## Spiny sleeper

Spiny sleepers were a very rare species of fish found in the Great Glacier.

Description
This plump fish was covered in tiny silver spines and had a white belly and long whiskers resembling a catfish. Handling the sleeper without proper care, such as not wearing gloves or protective gear, may result in one of the spines piercing the flesh and injecting a potent toxin. If the affected, they experienced effects similar to a temporal stasis spell, and their body would be covered in an ice-like glaze. The effect could be negated by neutralize poison or similar spells, as well as specific herbal treatments known by certain Ulutiun healers.

Behavior
Spiny sleepers displayed non-aggressive behavior, swiftly swimming away from any perceived threats.

Ecology
Spiny sleepers were scavengers. They consumed plankton, organic waste and any organic material.

Habitat
Spiny sleepers inhabited the rivers and seas of the Great Glacier. Spiny sleepers were not territorial and had a natural inclination to form schools of different sizes. Their reproductive strategy involved laying numerous tiny eggs, typically numbering in the thousands.

In the late 14 century DR, the hin thief named Jonslyn Windtongue managed to steal one of the spellwebs. The Ayrorch, the Council of Twelve, dispatched the Hands of Gond agents to find the halfling thief and return what was stolen by all means.

## Spaw

Sovereign Spaw was the ruler of Ebonlake Grotto, a myconid community in the Underdark situated near the Ebonlake.

Abilities
Spaw could communicate to non-myconid by use of spore clouds that lingered around Ebonlake Grotto.

It also had the ability to raise the bodies of the dead as spore servants, zombie-like husks that served as mindless followers and protectors.

Possessions
Spaw kept a treasury in Ebonlake Grotto, which house the powerful drow item, Shadow of Menzoberranzan.

History
In the, duergars aligned with the cult of the Absolute raided the Ebonlake Grotto and slew many of the myconid's young. Spaw raised several of the raiders as spore servants and sought out ways to take out vengeance on the cult and its duergar slavers. Spaw also offered safe haven to the gnome woman Thulla, who was grievously injured by the raiders.

The duergar also attacked the myconid sovereign Glut, and destroyed its entire circle. Spaw received an appeal for aid from Glut, but chose to ignore its requests outright.

## Spore servant

Spore servants were the animated remains of the dead, given a second existence by myconid sovereigns.

Description
Most once-living organic creatures could be transformed into a spore servant after their death. Exceptions included non-living entities, such as constructs or undead, or those without flesh and blood, including plants, elementals, and oozes.

Spore servants had a lifespan of anywhere from two to five weeks, or until they were destroyed. Once destroyed, the body of a spore servant could not be animated once again.

Abilities
Spore servants lost all spoken forms of communication they had in life. They could still communicate via rapport spores that were utilized by myconids and their sovereigns.

Combat
Spore servants would try to bludgeon or otherwise assail any creature that threatened its creator, or other allied myconids. They did not retain any specialized combat abilities or powers beyond attacking with their limbs, claws, tails, etc.

Ecology
While the process of creating a spore servant was near instantaneous, the gestation period took up to 24 hours.

History
When the demon lord Zuggtmoy orchestrated her "marriage" to Araumycos around the, she cast her spores all across the Underdark, creating scores of spore servants.

As of the, numerous spore servants of duergar, hook horrors, and other Underdark denizens could be found roaming around Ebonlake Grotto.

## Spellscarred savant

Spellscarred savants, sometimes called Spellplague disciples, were spellscarred individuals who had learned to harness the incredible power of their spellscars, allowing them to tap into the disruptive energies of the Spellplague as well as the raw power of spellfire.

Culture
Such individuals used their spellscars as weapons, making them among the strongest among the spellscarred. A spellscarred savant rejected the stigma or shame associated with having a spellscar, and instead regarded their scar as a powerful boon. Some would display their scars openly as a badge of honor or as a warning to their foes.

Only those who had the mental discipline and the courageous willingness to study their spellscar and the Spellplague could become a spellscarred savant.

Abilities
Spellscarred savants learned to familiarize themselves with the Spellplague in order to gain mastery over it, and thus gained the ability to bend these infamously chaotic powers to their will. While an ordinary spellscarred individual was vulnerable to the power of the Spellplague and of plaguechanged creatures, spellscarred savants learned not only to overcome the weaknesses to these energies, but to gain power over them.

As they grew in mastery, spellscarred savants gained the ability to tap into raw spellfire, which they could use to heal and empower themselves. Some used this power to expand the arsenal of attacks and powers arising from their spellscar while others used it to develop a remarkable immunity to fire. These savants came to regard mundane fire almost as a toy, and could freely extinguish or heal any fire on their person.

With continued practice, spellscarred savants came to be able to use spellfire to recharge their spellscar's powers, and as they gained mastery over their spellfire, they were said to wield the raw essence of magic as easily as a warrior wielded a sword. Such masters could incinerate their foes to ash and could replicate the powers of nearby arcane spellcasters.

History
Spellscarred savants only appeared following the outbreak of the Spellplague in the . By the, Spellplague disciples would be sought out by the Order of Blue Flame in order to recruit them as potential accordants.

## Spirecoast

Spirecoast was an ancient Netherese outpost built in the Sword Mountains during its early period of expansion.

Geography
The outpost was located within the foothills of the southern Sword Mountains, west of what would become the Westwood.

History
Spirecoast came about during Netheril's Silver Age ( to ), when the Terraseer directed Netheril to explore its western frontier. Reported among the first promising sites in, alongside Old Owl Well and Kryptgarden Falls, Spirecoast served as a caravan stop, watering station, and fortified watchpost amid Netheril's westward expansion. Secured with warriors and overseen by arcanists, it was equipped with crystal balls that were used to spy on the surrounding area, especially the nearby elves of Illefarn. The outpost was also fitted with a mythallar that powered quasimagical items, but the need for expeditions to venture further and further afield caused the creation of some of Netheril's first proper magical items that could be used anywhere.

## Spellstar

Spellstars were gems or metal star-shapes within which were stored spells. By touching a spellstar and whispering its command word, their users could release the spells kept inside.

Description
Spellstars themselves came in two varieties: those made of gemstone and those made of metal. The two were identical in function, simply different in appearance and cost. A spellstar made from a gem was flawless and glassy, of either blue or green color, and primarily made using a sapphire or an emerald, respectively. The gems used in this process had to be worth at least 6,000 gp. Conversely, a metal spellstar had to be made from purified steel and have a star-like shape. This steel must also be everbright, protecting the spellstar from oxidation and tarnishing.

As each spell within a spellstar was used, the spellstar would itself begin to shrink, melting away until, when the last one was released, it crumbled into an unrecoverable dust. The only way to recharge them was through the use of esoteric spells, and it was believed only three individuals knew the spells necessary: Alustriel Silverhand, the High Lady of Silverymoon; Syluné, the Witch of Shadowdale; and Elminster, the Sage of Shadowdale.

Powers
A spellstar served as a spell repository, holding a maximum of four within it. These were of either arcane or divine sources, but not both, and, if being sold, the number of spells contained within each gem or starshape also affected their price. Each spell stored had a unique command word assigned to it, which, if spoken while in contact with the spellstar, then unleashed it. In the moment of casting, the spellstar mentally passed onto the activator all of the spell's details, which then allowed the user to direct the magic as if they were casting it themselves. These spells, however, all functioned using the power and ability of the one who stored them in the spellstar initially.

Notable Owners
 The Tall Ones, Alustriel's twelve sons and pegasi-riding agents.

Appendix
References

---

## Speaking gem

A speaking gem or ahlrekauth was an enchanted gem made by elves that allowed them to communicate at a distance, and which held voice messages from ancient times. They were all but unknown by the late 14 century DR.

Powers
The most ancient of ahlrekauth were elven voice records, some of them more ancient than some celestial bodies. The process to awaken their voices, however, was esoteric even among the magically accomplished loremasters. One particular speaking-gem was made of malachite.

On Evermeet, a different kind of speaking stone was used to communicate.

History
Ahlrekauth were some of the most ancient sources known to have been created by elves. One particular pair of ancient speaking stones in Evermeet was keyed to Chandrelle Durothil and to Brindarry Nierde; Chandrelle's stone belonged to her father.

Until, the song dragon Ammaratha Cyndusk kept a collection of over 4,000 speaking gems in a fortress hidden within Claw Peak. She traded those to other dragons, including Aeglyl Dreadclaw, in exchange for them sparing the life of Vangerdahast, the Royal Mage of Cormyr.

Reputation
By the 14 century, even Vangerdahast had no idea what those were, but they were greatly appreciated by dragons.

Notable Owners
 Ammaratha Cyndusk
 Aeglyl Dreadclaw
 Brindarry Nierde
 Chandrelle Durothil

## Sparks Musgravel

Sparks Musgravel was a member of The Guild from the Lower City of Baldur's Gate in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Sparks was a middle-aged human man of light complexion. He had green eyes and wavy blonde hair. Like other grunts of the Guild, Sparks wore green-colored wizardly garb and carried a simple staff.

History
In the, Sparks Musgravel was tasked with standing guard of the secret entrance into Baldur's Gate's Undercellar where The Guild's Guildhall was hidden. Whilst a half-orcish enforcer Tusgront manned the main entrance, Sparks was ready to dispatch of any unwanted guest from a distance.

## Sprite language

The Sprite language was the racial language of sprites, a type of fey.

Speakers
Beyond sprites themselves, Sprite was a common secondary language in Faerûn among forest gnomes, lightfoot halflings, rock gnomes, and tallfellow halflings. Beyond demihumans, Sprite was also commonly spoken by their fellow fey classified as sprites, the atomies, grigs, and pixies. Non-sprite fey known for speaking the language included brownies, dobies, dryads and hamadryads, and gorse faeries. Beyond sylvan creatures, the Sprite language was commonly known by faerie dragons.

Sprite was also fairly common as a secondary language among most druids, including those devoted to certain nature deities. These included the druids of Chauntea, druids of Eldath, druids and druid/rangers of Mielikki, the druids of Silvanus, and the druids of Sheela Peryroyl. Other forms of specialty priests in the Realms for whom Sprite was a commonly known secondary language were the Eldathyn peacemen.

Notable Speakers
 Arkaneus Silvermane, the Archdruid of Mosstone.
 Endeara, an ancient treant that lived along the Vesper River.
 Lewis Bookreader I, a human that ran the Well-Read Head bookstore in Ravens Bluff in the late 14 century DR.
 Peppercorn, a faerie dragon that lived in the For-Rest Inn in Ravens Bluff in the late 14 century DR.
 Thistledew Vine, the dryad innkeeper of the For-Rest Inn.

## Spoon

Spoons were a kind of utensil used in cooking and eating, widely appreciated for their scooping and stirring qualities.

Description
The average spoon was used in kitchens and dining rooms for preparing and eating food, alongside forks and knives. They were also a standard part of mess kits used by travelers. Their most important feature was the bowl-like protuberance that allowed them to scoop up liquid foods like soup and porridge. Cheap dining spoons were commonly made from brass, while fancier ones were crafted from silver. Spoons used in cooking were often made of wood.

Spades and shovels were essentially massive spoons used for farming, mining, and other labor-intensive work. Ladles were more modest, resembling oversized spoons used to prepare and serve food.

Usage
Magic
 Mordenkainen's magnificent mansion required a silver spoon.

Notable Spoons
 Murlynd's spoon, a magical spoon that produced gruel.
 One of the known types of Quaal's feather tokens was a spoon.
 Utensils of the cultured palate, magical sets of eating utensils that always included a spoon.

## Spatula

Spatulas, referred to as osaye in Wa-an, were a type of tool found across Toril, including the ancient Calim Empire, the Vast, and on Wa.

Description
Spatulas were defined by their large, flat heads that allowed them to carry food and other substances.

Usage
Spatulas were commonly used as cookware across the Realms.

In Wa, a special metal-tipped osaye could be used to serve powdered incense during an incense ceremony.

Spatulas were also used as a tool for spreading non-food pastes and plasters, similar to trowels.

History
Sometime in the 14 century DR, the cargo ship The Gentle Lady was delivering a shipment of farmer and merchant goods to a small coastal community in the Dragon Reach. Among the goods being carried by the ship was a crate full of cookware that included eighteen spatulas.

## Spike

Spikes were large metal rods that were pointed at one end (essentially giant nails), used in carpentry, construction, and adventuring. They belonged to a class of hardware that also included bolts and pegs, and were very similar to pitons.

Description
Spikes were most often between  and  long, made from iron with a square cross-section. Depending on their purpose they could be made with a simple flattened head, or with an eyehole for looping a rope, carabiner, or some other device.

Aside from bolts, nails, and pegs, spikes also had a resemblance to stakes.

Availability
Nails were made by blacksmiths as part of their regular work.

Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue offered a selection of spikes and related tools. Spikes could be had in  (3 gp/dozen) and  (5 gp/dozen) lengths.

Usage
When used for joining wood in carpentry, the spike could be set using an auger to make a shallow indentation, or have a hole partially bored out with a bow drill. Once the surface was prepared, the spike could be suitably struck with a hammer or mallet.

Eyeholed spikes were a vital part of a climber's tools, as they could be driven into stone to provide attachment points for rope. Spikes built specifically for climbing were generally known as pitons.

Adventurers found ready uses for spikes beyond climbing, with the most obvious being that of spiking doors shut to prevent unexpected entry, and wedging them open to ensure easy access. Because of the sheer size and durability, a large spike was a perfectly acceptable emergency weapon, easily capable of clubbing or impaling a target. If nothing else, they could simply be thrown at suspected traps to detect or trigger them safely.

Magic
Hold monster used a metal rod as a material component, such as a nail or spike.

The statue spell could be cast using a mixture of lime, sand, and water stirred by an iron nail or spike.

Notable Spikes
 Blast spike, an explosive dwarven mining tool.

## Spiral

Spiral was one of the "worldlets" that existed in the same crystal sphere as the Rock of Bral.

Description
Spiral had winding rivers that flowed from the polar seas to the equatorial ocean and back. It was also said to have a little orange sun that, possibly, orbited around the Spiral itself.

The planet was once inhabited by elves, both warriors and farmers, hence Spiral probably had some fertile lands as well as the already mentioned rivers, polar seas, and equatorial ocean. It also had deep caves and mountains, where the elves hid when Spiral was attacked by the scro Tarantula fleet.

## Spinning top

A spinning top, or simply a top, was a type of toy in the Realms that had a squat body and sharp pointed bottom, designed to be spun on its vertical axis and balance on its tip while doing so.

Description
Tops could be made from a variety of different metals, including brass, bronze, and copper. Among the Iulutiuns of the Great Glacier, they were commonly made of either ice or wood.

Varieties
Singing tops A variety of spinning top from the land of Amn. These tops were ringed with holes that caused them to emit a sharp whistling sound as they spun.

Availability
 Aurora's Whole Realms Catalogue sold both solid and singing tops imported from Amn under its "Diversions" section. Advertised as being "perfectly balanced", these tops were various made of brass, bronze, and copper. Each could be bought for 5 silver pieces each or for the price of 2 gold pieces a customer could order a mixed variety of five tops.
 In the Domain of Barovia, the toysmith Gadof Blinsky of Vallaki sold wooden spinning tops that were painted with a scene of scarecrows chasing children through a forest.
 Ruvya's Toys and Amusements, a toyshop in Bezantur, sold spinning tops.

Usage
The wu-jen version of the spell Whirlwind required a spinning top as a material component.

Notable Owners
 Nicos Corynian kept a table exclusively for spinning tops in his home's game room in Luthcheq. They were part of a game where one was meant to use them to try and knock down arrangements of little wooden pins.

Appendix
See Also
 Gigwhorl, a Calishite thieves tool that operated on a similar principle.

## Spell rebirth

Spell rebirth was an advanced utterance from the Lexicon of the Evolving Mind that used truespeak to reactivate a dispelled spell, or actually dispelled it, if reversed.

Usage
In its straight form, spell rebirth unwound a lost spell back to existence, if its duration had not completely elapsed. In its reversed form, it dispelled spells.

History
The recitation was known to loquasphinxes and garblers, the latter using only its reversed form.

## Speed of the zephyr

Speed of the zephyr was an utterance from the Lexicon of the Evolving Mind that used truespeak that granted agility and speed without match for a moment, or in its reversed form, reduced speed.

Usage
In its straight form, it granted a great boost to speed and the ability to walk on liquids or through even the most obstructed terrain with no challenge, even up walls for the seconds it lasted. In its reversed form, it reduced speed somewhat, making their feet feel as though they stuck to the ground.

History
The utterance was known to garblers, in its reversed form, and loquasphinxes.

## Spelljammer Academy

Spelljammer Academy was the secret headquarters of the Spelljammer Fleet, located in Nimbral.

Location
The Academy was located off the west coast of Nimbral, in the Trackless Sea.

Structure
Nine stories tall, the Academy was carved into a natural stone pillar  tall. The exterior was covered in walkways and balconies. An open bowl in the top of the pillar acted as a dock for spelljammers, mainly ones operated by the Spelljammer Fleet, but independent merchant spelljammers were not uncommon.

Interior
Each level of Spelljammer Academy is dedicated to a specific role. From top to bottom, these were a sea dock, administration, stores, cadet quarters, gymnasium, simulations deck, spelljammer nexus, bridge quarters, and spelljammer dock.

The sea dock was a cavern at the base of the pillar in which boats could unload personnel, visitors, equipment, supplies, and animals, and acted as the primary entrance to the Academy.

Administration included a reception area, visitor quarters, a lounge, and the facility's record room. This was the highest level a visitor could enter.

The stores were where the equipment, weapons, and rations used by the Academy were stored.

The cadet quarters included separate sleeping areas for cadets and graduates, a laundry, a washroom, the Weeping Goddess, a kitchen, and a refectory. The Weeping Goddess was a raucous bar that serviced the cadets at the Academy.

The gymnasium included multiple spaces for physical activity, as well as a washroom.

The simulation chambers were rooms able to create realistic environments using illusion magic, in which new recruits could be trained in a safe environment under the watch of their teachers.

The spelljammer nexus included a laboratory, a library of astronomical references, and the spelljammer nexus itself: A chamber containing a  tall orrery of Realmspace surrounded by eight training spelljamming helms.

The bridge quarters were the living spaces of the head staff that run the Academy. This included a private banquet chamber, a spellbook library, and a communication room called the oracularium.

The sky dock was the top level. It was open to the sky and operated as a dock for the Spelljammer Fleet's spelljammers.

Activities
Spelljammer Academy trained all manner of races from Toril and the larger universe to operate spelljammers and to fight to protect Toril from threats in the Astral Sea. This included the Corps and Spelljammer training tracks. Corps cadets were trained in melee and ranged weapons, whereas Spelljammer Cadets were trained in navigation and piloting of spelljammers.

Spelljammers were occasionally dispatched from the Academy in response to threats to Toril in Realmspace. Three out of ten such missions ended with all hands lost.

Defenses
Different areas required security clearance to enter. Each clearance was indicated by uniform color. These clearances, in ascending order, were Visitor (Green), Cadet (Red), Sailor (Blue), Officer (Purple), and Bridge (Gold).

History
Spelljammer Academy was founded by Mirt the Merciless in a joint venture with Elmandar, who owned a shop on the Rock of Bral. Prince Andru invested in Spelljammer Academy, but reduced funding over questions of security.

Inhabitants
 Mirt the Merciless, Academy Head
 Boatswain Tarto, Spelljammer Corps Training Officer
 Sor'kur, Receptionist
 Kip Whistleslap, Ship Inspector
 Pik Whistleslap, Ship Inspector
 Saerthe Abizjn, Spelljammer Training Officer
 Mister Blip, Academy Quartermaster
 Petty Officer Winston Ryeback, Cook

## Spellthief

A spellthief was an individual trained in stealth and practiced in stealing the magical powers of others.

Abilities
Though spellthieves were much like rogues at heart, they possessed the unique power to steal spells from those they snuck; with progress, they were able to progressively steal more and more complex spells. Developing this power made their abilities slightly different from those of other sneaks: they developed the ability to cast detect magic on their own and had an inexplicable aptitude for thwarting the worst of any spell's effect.

Spellthieves soon learned to steal ongoing spell effects from others, and then commit ever stranger thefts: they were known to steal resistance against some kinds of energy and, as their talents progressed in the power of the spells they could steal, they also gained the ability to steal the spells of magical creatures, not only those of wizards and others. A spellthief progressing in talent could also steal a spell for their own use instead of being affected by it. Advanced spellthieves developed an ability akin to arcane sight, which they could use at least once per day, or as often as their sense of self was able to power it. They also gained a very limited selection of arcane spells of their own, limited only to those of lesser or middling power, and never from necromancy, evocation, or conjuration.

Even more powerful spellthieves could instantly discover all of a mage's prepared spells simply by stealing one spell; those nearing the pinnacle of the path's possibilities were also able to snatch away their foes' spell resistance when attacking by surprise.

History
After the Time of Troubles in 1358 DR, mortals who came into contact with gods' blood were changed by the ichor; some of them became spellthieves, though the changes sometimes took a generation or so to take effect. The spellthieves whose power came from godsblood were sometimes linked intrinsically to these powers, and had a strange ability to cast divine magic from these gods' domains with stolen spell power.

Other mortals gained spellthieving powers from wild magic exposure, or tainted water such as from the Winding Water.

Notable Spellthieves
 Anwhar Bellgate
 Erilon Amadar

Spider dart was a type of dart used by the drow.
Powers
When a living target was struck with this dart, it released a poison. In addition, upon impact, the dart itself transformed into a large spider. The spider acted independently and attacked indiscriminately.

## Spirit arrow

Spirit arrows were a variety of magical arrows made or used by barbarians that could penetrate most magical defenses.

Description
These arrows were rather mundane in appearance, though always had an arrowhead made of white flint.

Powers
Spirit arrows had the power to outright ignore and pentrate most protective spells, such as fire shield mage armor, or stoneskin. They also could bypass the magical defenses offered by magical armor or clothing, such as cloaks of protection, bracers of defense, and rings of protection. Once a spirit arrow landed, regardless of whether it struck a target or not, they became destroyed.

History
Sometime in the mid-14 century DR, a group of adventurers in Ravens Bluff found themselves magically transported by a painting to the land of Chult when searching for Cuthbert Maguluty. In order to return home they had to retrieve a magical painting depicting Rupertyna Phyneus-Maguluty's cottage that had been stolen by ptera-men. While at the village of the ptera-men, the adventurers found a small treasure hoard within the hut of their chieftain, containing such items as a mask of beastliness, ten spirit arrows, sixteen emeralds, and a dagger with a solid emerald pommel.

## Spell mirror (item)

A spell mirror was an enchanted protective device.

Description
Spell mirrors were ovoid devices made of polished glass.

Powers
A spell mirror was keyed against a single spell, and deflected any instance of that spell aimed at the one carrying it away. The deflection was always somewhere past them, equidistant from the point of origin, and at an angle of 45 degrees.

Though the spell mirror had no limit on the spells it could reflect, there was a significant chance, about 1 in 12, of it crumbling away into dust, releasing both a blast of damaging force and an angry creature bound to the mirror, which would inevitably turn on the bearer.

Creatures bound up in such mirrors included firestars, will-o'-wisps, or volts.

History
These mirrors were common in Oerth, where they were as old as the practice of wizardry. Mordenkainen once brought one to a moot with Elminster Aumar and Dalamar on Earth.

Appendix
References

## Spell fetish

Spell fetishes were the spellcasting implements used by the arcane and some divine spellcasters of the Malatran Plateau in Kara-Tur. In lieu of spellbooks and material components that did not exist on the Plateau, wizards used various effigies, totems, and trinkets to store the knowledge of the magic in. This unique phenomenon that made Malatran magic different from anywhere else on Toril was due to an ancient and mysterious Pact of the Ancients and the fact that all magic was drawn from and fueled by elemental and spirits.

Description
Spell fetishes were as varied as the spells they contained, from pretty rocks to intricate carvings and poppets. Instead of pouring over their spellbooks, Malatran arcane casters nightly meditated over the selected fetishes to memorize their magical incantations and formulae. Divine magics were sometimes channeled through similar fetishes that could act like a divine focus – a holy symbol crafted out of rocks, bones, skins, feathers and other jungle materials, or be more artistically complex carvings, consumed with use. Such divine fetishes could have a single use or a set number of casting and with each use, the fetish slowly decayed from the inside, eventually becoming fragile and crumbling to dust.

The Kukalatu tribe had a more unique approach to fetishes, of fi in the Kukalatu language. They exclusively used bones, teeth, and hair of the deceased tribe members, usually respected individuals or elders. The older the ancestral fetishes were, the more powerful they became.

Some fetishes required an olfactory component of meditation. Such charms as the stinking cloud spell fetish, made of a broken shell of a rotten egg, or the sleep spell fetish made with died nemuri within a gourd, required the spellcaster to inhale the smells of the fetishes during their meditation. Others required the caster to meditate on mental images and imagining of certain scenarios. For example, the enlarge spell fetish depicted a carving of a fat Nubari and while meditating, the caster needed to focus on the man's size and imagine how he came to be so large.

Some arcane spellcasters of the Living Jungle were able to tap into dark necromantic magics, despite generally spells of the school not functioning on the Malatran Plateau. Headshrinkers, Malatran necromancers, learned to side-step the use of spirit magic of the fetishes by creating phantorrens, a type pf enchanted container that contained spirits drained from living humanoids. This device was limited to the spells of the schools of necromancy and alteration.

## Spider cloak

A spider cloak was an enchanted cloak that likely fell into the possession of several adventurers in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Such cloaks were dark red and had a brown spiderweb embroidered on their backs. The hood of the cloak was also decorated in a similar way.

Powers
It allowed its user to hide from opponents and avoid their successful attacks. However, this ability had varying recovery periods before it could be used again.

History
In the late 15 century DR, spider cloaks could be found in Neverwinter, Gauntlgrym, Mount Hotenow, and Neverwinter Wood. They were also available for purchase at shops in those locations. Such items were likely found by the adventurers that stopped Deathsleep, the green dragon.

## Spirelli

Spirelli was a giant talking snail who was the only non-human member of the Blade Council of Lomatra in the early 13 century DR.

Description
Spirelli was a human-sized snail. His eyes were on the ends of prehensile eyestalks, which had the habit of swiveling to look at both sides of an argument at once.

Bubbles foamed under his shell when he was upset.

When in battle, he rode on the back of a horse and wore a helmet.

Personality
Spirelli was a very charismatic individual and a persuasive speaker. He could also be underhanded and conniving, using his wealth to further his own ends.

Possessions
Spirelli was extremely wealthy. He owned two palaces, a golden pleasure barge, and a large mercenary company. He was the owner of about half of Lomatra in the Blade Kingdoms, including the city hall. He was known to travel in a sedan chair.

History
Spirelli was not originally from Lomatra, although his exact origins were unclear. According to Lorenzo Utrelli, he came to Lomatra around the  from a forest that could have been the Chondalwood. He originally went by a different name, which sounded like Boble-boop. He brought with him a large supply of mother of pearl, which he exchanged for considerable wealth. Following his arrival in Lomatra, Spirelli started paying taxes, which entitled him to citizenship. Over a year later, he became a mercenary commander by hiring a large mercenary company. He spent nearly 200,000 ducats becoming a Blade Captain.

In the winter of the, Spirelli made a late appearance at the meeting of the Blade Council of Lomatra. He argued in favor of Lomatra remaining allies with Sumbria. In their next meeting his vote was interrupted by Miliana Mannicci and Lorenzo Utrelli who used a scheme involving peppercorns to outvote him.

Some weeks later, Spirelli rode alongside Orlando Tropello in the aftermath of the battle with Ugo Svarézi, and failed to buy the favor of Orlando or his soldiers.

## Spider's Fleet o' Feet

Spider's Fleet o' Feet was a courier business in Lower Port, Skullport.

Description
Spider's Fleet o' Feet was owned by Spider.

Services
Spider's Fleet o' Feet offered a range of courier services. The cost for delivering verbal messages, limited to 50 to 100 words, was 1 cp per word. Written letters and personal missives were priced between 5 sp and 5 gp, while small package deliveries ranged from 1 to 10 gp. Should Spider decided the delivery was particularly dangerous or risky for his couriers, the cost of the service doubled or even tripled, reflecting the added peril involved in the task. Spider's delivery network was highly regarded in Skullport for its efficiency and reliability, despite the dangerous environment in which it operated.

To ensure the success of these deliveries and to protect his crew from potential threats, Spider employed a strategic system: he always sent two additional runners beyond the required number. These extra couriers acted as decoys or unseen escorts, providing cover for the real courier.

Spider's couriers were all fighters and rogues. They typically wore leather armor or none at all, and they were equipped with short swords and daggers. Some of these couriers acquired, or stole, magical items—such as gauntlets of climbing, bracers of brachiation, or rings of free action—to assist them in their duties.

## Speitar Dun'Robar

Speitar Dun'Robar was a skilled drow Blademaster of House Dun'Robar that fought in the Sschindylryn invasion of Eveningstar and the hunt for the Thread of the Weave during the Darkening in the 1480s DR.

Description
Speitar wore his hair in a mohawk.

Personality
Speitar had a grandiose self-perception, considering himself an unsurpassed fighter.

History

During the Darkening, Speitar served Vicala Szind as an officer when their forces struck the outlying farmsteads around Eveningstar while other drow attacked the village proper. Their surprise attack captured the Midler farmhouse and the entire Midler family, who were held hostage in their own home and interrogated by Vicala for information about the Thread of the Weave. Speitar handled the lesser drow while they waited, who were dissatisfied with the lack of combat and treasure to be found, but he and his forces were overcome by a group of adventurers sent to rescue the family.

## Spinach

Spinach was a variety of a green leafy vegetable found in some areas of Faerûn and primarily used as food.

Availability
Spinach was a staple crop in most family-run farms in Sembia, often being grown in the same farm plot as other vegetables.

Usages
Spinach was used as a type of pie filling in Cormyr and in the city of Neverwinter, it was used for spinach quiches.

Runsun was a variety of common trail food—a pie made with meat cooked in spinach-and-mint or spinach-and-hot-pepper simmer sauce.

Notable Users
 Spinach was served at the Elfsong Tavern of Baldur's Gate.

History
In the late 14 century DR, Dradeel's Vegetable Surprise, made with spinach, was a recipe developed by Dradeel, a mad elvish mage and a survivor of Balduran's final voyage to the isles of Anchorome.

In the, Emmeline Hallowleaf, mother to Shadowheart, one of the Absolute Adversaries, knew a recipe for Feast Day Cheese Bake made with various vegetables, including spinach, which she may have had shared with her daughter once the war with The Absolute was over.

Splatters Doolug was a mural painter from the Lower City of Baldur's Gate in the late 15 century DR.

Doolug was a half-orcish man of a large frame with a rough orcish face, curly blonde hair, and a muddy light green hide. He often wore an artist's garb and a feathered beret.

History
In the, during the Absolute crisis, Splatters Doolug spent his days painting murals on the Old Wall. His art faced criticism from some passersby Baldurians, like Slatey Marven, who thought Doolug took criticism poorly.

## Spotted mushroom

Spotted mushroom was a species of fungus used in cooking on the Sword Coast North, gathered in Neverwinter Wood, notable for its taste and red cap.

History
In the late 15 century DR, Jingo Rummage, a halfling adventurer, gathered forest herbs in Neverwinter Wood when his companion Jodri was eaten by wild wolf-men. As revenge, Jingo asked a group of Neverwintran adventurers to gather umber leaves, spotted mushrooms, and spiceroot, to add them to the wolves' cooking pots. The aroma attracted hungry ogres to the camp, who, in turn, slaughtered the beast-men.

## Space Lairs

Space Lairs is a 2-edition Advanced Dungeons & Dragons accessory. It is part of the Spelljammer campaign setting.

Description
Space Lairs was the last installment of Spelljammer Reference (SJR) series of books and the second-to-last book in AD&D's line of Spelljammer books. It was written by Nicky Rea and Wes Nicholson, with cover and interior art by David O. Miller, and cartography by John Knecht. It is an anthology of fourteen mini-adventures, with most running 3-4 pages long, designed to be easily inserted into any Spelljammer campaign. Arranged in descending order of difficulty, these encounters are designed to accommodate a broad range of player levels. In a manner similar to Book of Lairs and Book of Lairs II, each mini-adventure is predicated by a "Setup", followed by a "Lair", with each being related to a specific monster. One of these adventures, "Dwellers in the Vortex", has a connection to the Forgotten Realms through the Shou.

Contents
 How to use Space Lairs
 The Good Ship Gump (Colossus)
 Deadly Riddles (Astrosphinx)
 The Deathspider (Syllix)
 A Breath of Fresh Air (Phlog-crawler)
 The Scam (Space Mimic)
 The Iron Hive (Clockwork Horrors)
 Up, Up, and Away (Puffers)
 Join Us (Survivor)
 Marooned (Vampires)
 Pirates of the Shattered Forge (Pirates of Gith)
 A Lair of Flame (Firelich)
 Dwellers in the Vortex (Radiant Golem)
 The Mortiss Ship (Stellar Undead)
 Base of Woes
Extras
 Rock of Bral Poster

## Spectral axe

Spectral axe was a spell known to Rilder Palengerrast in the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Effects
The spell formed an axe made of force that struck at the caster's direction.

Components
The spell was known to have verbal components; it may have had others.

History
In the, the apprentice Red Wizard Rilder Palengerrast had this as his strongest battle spell. He tried to use it against the Simbul, expecting it may work where magic missile didn't work, but was not able to confirm its effectiveness, because he used it to strike her projected image instead.

## Spellsnaring sphere

Spellsnaring sphere was one of the Simbul's personal spells during the mid-to-late 14 century DR.

Effects
The spellsnaring sphere created an area where the caster was able to wrest control of others' spells away from their casters, keeping them from effectively ending their own spells, and prevented even other quelling spells from affecting the targeted magics. For spells that spared the caster from their effects, it also made them subject to its effects too. The spell also allowed even ordinarily fixed or stationary spell effects to be moved around.

History
The Simbul used this spell, one of her newer ones, while assaulting the stronghold of the Red Wizard Azmyrandyr in the, after she was attacked by Sharran priests during her raid. She employed this spell to hijack a blade barrier, which she proceeded to send forth in pursuit of other foes, also preventing its dismissal by the priest who cast it.

## Spider of Shar

Spider of Shar was a divine spell used by priests of Shar.

Effects
The spider of Shar spell created a cloud of darkness overhead, which projected tendrils down. Those tendrils whipped around, bringing stinging pain; they could also twist limbs, or even heads.

Components
The spell involved motions, and thus had a somatic component. Other components may have been involved.

History
A casting of spider of Shar was employed against the Simbul by the Sharran priest Staenyn in  during her raid on the stronghold of Azmyrandyr, a Red Wizard. He aimed it at her projected image, however, and only managed one slaying by friendly fire: the Red Wizard Marlus Belraeblood, whose head was twisted off by the spell.

## Spirit fetch

Spirit fetch was a ritual that summoned a "fetch"—a small and insubstantial spiritual servant—to coerce another person or creature.

Effects
The caster of the ritual chose whether to summon a fey, primal, or shadow fetch to serve them, as well as specified the shape of the fetch (generally they took the form of a small and indistinct animal or humanoid). The caster then whispered a command of up to 10 words that they wished for the fetch to deliver to a creature or creatures, at which point it sped away to find its target(s). Upon finding the target(s), the fetch's mere presence served to communicate the caster's command (the fetch was otherwise unable to interact or speak). The target(s) were not compelled to obey the command, however the fetch would linger at their side as a constant reminder until they obeyed or until the ritual expired (which could be up to a month depending on the skill of the caster). A fetch could not be harmed or destroyed, although casting dispel magic could drive it away for 24 hours.

Because of the fetch's ability to seek out its target(s), the ritual was also useful simply for locating the target(s) rather than coercing them. However, some methods of warding against divination could render a creature unable to be found by a fetch, and could cause the fetch to seek them in the wrong direction.

Components
The ritual entailed 20 gp worth of material component and took ten minutes to enact. In addition, it required the caster to be able to identify their target(s), either by naming them, by visualizing their specific appearance, or by providing a physical sample of them (such as their blood).

Users
Spirit fetch was said to be a tool used by the Vistani.

The feywardens of Sarifal trained to be able to perform the ritual once each day without needing any material components.

## Spirits of the shadowed moon

Spirits of the shadowed moon was a spell known to shamans that allowed them to summon a primal spirit of the moon.

Effects
The moon spirit burst forth from the summoner and appeared as a creature of mist, shadow, and glimmering lights. The spirit concealed the summoner and any allies within, and could move freely if the summoner willed it.

A spirit could be summoned only once per day, and would aid the summoner for the duration of a battle.

History
Among the feywardens of Sarifal were those who embraced the trickery of the Feywild and could gain the ability to replicate this spell.

## Spiritualist

A spiritualist was a type of psion who, rather than specializing in one of the disciplines, devoted themselves to an interest in death, undeath, and the afterlife. They honed their psionic powers towards such ends, learning how to sense and at times even control the spirits of the deceased. They were equivalent to the necromancers of arcane magic.

Activities
Spiritualists frequently attempted to contact spirits of the deceased, an occultic practice that they gained a reputation for. They also were often sought out by others for divination purposes.

Culture
Spiritualists were typified as rather enigmatic and mysterious individuals, in many cases even being of evil alignments. It was not uncommon for them to speak to others in the form of riddles, verse, or even roundabout phrases.

Unlike other psionicists, they were often found to be well versed in manners of ancient history, astrology, disguises, fortune telling, healing, and religion.

Spiritualist were found be active in Cormyr, Sembia, and Waterdeep. They tended to be viewed in the same suspicious light as necromancers or witches, even in such aforementioned areas of the Realms that were considered to be relatively civilized and enlightened. Many nervous and superstitious commoners would bolt their doors or bar their windows at a spiritualist's passing, or even go so far as to chase them down with torches. Those who weren't superstitious likewise looked down upon them, perceiving their occult divinatory practices as dangerous wastes of time.

Prerequisites
The one prerequisite to being able to go down this path as a psionicist was knowing the power spirit sense.

Abilities
Spiritualists had no particular discipline that they favored over others, though they typically drew from clairsentience, psychokinesis, psychometabolism, and telepathy. Because of their strong links to the dead they were quite proficient in seances and had a spell-like ability to speak with dead once or more per week.

Powers commonly learned by spiritualists included animate shadow, awe, cause decay, cell adjustment, death field, double pain, ectoplasmic form, false sensory input, incarnation awareness, life detection, life draining, project force, psychic clone, psychic drain, psychic messenger, retrospection, sensitivity to psychic impressions, shadow-form, stasis field, suspend animation, synaptic static, and wrench.

Possessions
The preferred manner of dress for a spiritualist was typically a long, flowing and concealing robe, rather than more modern styles. If a spiritualist had any armor, it would be found worn beneath their robes. They almost always carried around mystical equipment for seances and fortune telling, largely for effect. This included such things as candles, cards, spirit boards, and most often a mundane or magical crystal ball.

## Spear of the mammoth

Spear of the mammoth was an enchanted spear created by Graendel Granitefist of Graendel's Fine Dwarven Craft in Targos in the late 14 century DR.

Powers
Spear of the mammoth was created from a magic +2 spear and a woolly mammoth's tusk. The weapon was imbued with the namesake animal's vigor and kinetic force. The finished spear possessed a potent +3 enchantment that guided the wielder's hand towards the target and made the weapon's damage more deadly. Additionally, it boosted the wielder's constitution and allowed the wielder to knock their opponents prone as if they were stricken by a charging mammoth.

History
Graendel Granitefist, in his old age, sought to create a collection of masterwork weapons and armor that would immortalize his dwarven craft. One of these items, forged in the, was a spear of the mammoth.

## Speckled Worthog Inn

The Speckled Worthog Inn was a fairly hospitable inn located somewhere along the Coast Road in the Vast in the late 14 century DR.

Structure
The inn's front windows were composed of stained glass, too opaque to see through. Hung over its front door was a weathered sign depicting a warthog with red spots that had faded over time. Behind the establishment stood a large barn for patron to stable their mounts.

Interior
The inn was made up of five rooms. There was a common room full of tables and a fireplace, a kitchen, a large bar area, and two rooms restricted to the owner and staff.
Atmosphere
The Speckled Worthog catered to a number of Vastian farmers, such as those from the nearby village of Farmersville.

Services
The inn charged a single silver piece for a night in their common room or a single gold piece for one of their more private rooms, which could fit up to three individuals, as well as two copper pieces for use of their stables. In terms of refreshments the Speckled Worthog offered an aromatic beef stew for 4 cp, cheap ale for 5 cp, good ale for 1 sp, and a glass of elven wine for 2 sp.

The inn also sold some basic equipment for adventurers, including horses at a price of 3 cp.

History
Sometime in the late 14 century DR, a few days after the Andonian Games, a group of adventurers from the city of Ravens Bluff were traveling the Coast Road to Tantras in search of Discount Merlin, a charlatan salesman of faulty magical items that the City Guard wanted brought to justice. Several hours after they had left Ravens Bluff, around the time of sunset, the adventurers came across the Speckled Worthog and decided to stay the night. At that time the bard Tangis Strongnote was staying at the inn, delighting patrons with songs and the strumming of his lyre.

The adventurers inquired about Discount Merlin with Tangis, the Speckled Worthog's staff, a dwarf traveler named Milo Finglerock, and a cleric of Moradin named Sophia Rockjumper. While there they learned that a gnome had visited the inn two days prior, hocking magical items, but did not match Merlin's description. He tried to interest Tangis in a magical lyre and managed to convince the proprietor to trade a couple bottles of elven wine for a rope of entanglement. Much like the items he had sold back in Ravens Bluff, the rope had since lost its enchantment.

Inhabitants
The Speckled Worthog was owned and operated by Nivla Worthog, a very loquacious individual who was quick to offer his patrons a meal or refill of a drink. He lived at the inn along with his daughter, Rose Worthog, who worked the kitchen, and her husband Milgelis.

The hatchery was set up by the Church of Lolth from Sschindylryn to breed spiders en masse for the service of Lolth, making the whole area a kind of religious site. The area was directly defended and supervised by the priestesses and their servants.

## Sphinx (ship)

The Sphix was a ship belonging to Captain Lukas that sank in Kork Bay in the Strait of the Leviathan in the late 1470s DR.

Description
The ship was fast and sturdy, made with broad beams and with masts crowded with sails. Below deck was cramped, with some one-person cabins.

The ship carried a skiff to be used as a lifeboat or for reaching land while the ship was anchored. This skiff contained several hidden compartments large enough to hide weapons.

Inhabitants
The crew of the Sphinx on its last voyage comprised:
 Captain Lukas, a human ranger from Loudwater who was a skilled fighter and tracker.
 Gaspar-shen, a watersoul genasi swordsman from Calimshan with a keen interest in pastry.
 Kip, a young cat-shifter with unique empathic abilities.
 Marikke, a priestess of Chauntea in her role as the Earthmother.
 The Savage, a golden elf swordmage dressed in black to hide his true nature as a daemonfey.
 Suka, a gnome with a foul mouth who specialized in concealed weapons.

History
The Sphinx was built in Baldur's Gate by Lukas himself, and when it arrived in the Moonshae Isles, it was one of the fastest ships yet seen there, leading to copies. For years, Lukas sailed the ship across the Trackless Sea, and everything he owned came to be stored on it.

After Lukas met Gaspar-shen in Alaron, the pair ran Amnian cargo between Snowdown and Callidyrr. Next to join was the Savage followed by Suka, who had witnessed the ship in awe from Llewellyn Harbor and pledged to join its crew. The last to join were Marikke and Kip, whom Lukas claimed they needed because of Marikke's healing powers, but in truth he may have simply wanted to help rescue them from the lives of poverty they were living in Caer Callidyrr. Marikke first met Lukas in a courthouse in Caer Callidyrr as he and Gaspar-shen attempted to negotiate the release of The Savage from prison. The Savage counseled against bringing Marikke aboard because he did not trust her, while Suka simply did not like her.

Soon after, about seven months after Lukas paid off the last of his debts incurred from building the ship, the crew incurred more debts to House Kendrick, the crown of Callidyrr, possibly relating to the Savage's stint in jail. They were offered a chance to waive this debt in exchange for taking Lord Aldon Kendrick, a cousin to the king, on a potentially dangerous mission to the ruins of Caer Corwell on Gwynneth to meet with the Winterglen Claw, a group of human rebels opposed to the fey of Sarifal who ruled the island. This would turn out to be a trap for Lord Aldon laid by a rival family member, and he wound up dead by the hand of the High Lady Ordalf, the queen of Sarifal. She, in turn, offered to hire the Sphinx for a mission to kill her half-sister, Amaranth, on Moray Island. In addition to offering a large sum of Amnian gold, Ordalf imprisoned Suka as an assurance of loyalty. However, she herself would immediately betray them upon realizing that Marikke and Kip together satisfied a prophecy held by Argon Bael and the Black Blood tribe of Moray, leading her to signal them as the Sphinx sailed along the coast.

Despite knowing terrible stories of fire nagas in the waters around Moray, Lukas gambled on a nighttime approach at the narrowest point between Moray and Gwynneth at Kork Head. Sure enough, the ship was attacked by the nagas, set aflame, and sunk to the bottom of Kork Bay a mere four days after setting out from Caer Corwell. Even so, the entire crew survived: Kip, Marikke, and the Savage reached the shore in the skiff while Gaspar-shen saved Lukas from drowning and swam ashore with him. The three aboard the skiff were immediately attacked and captured by Argon Bael and his lycanthrope followers, while Lukas and Gaspar-shen pursued them after retrieving their equipment stashed in the skiff.

The timbers of the Sphinx came to litter the beaches of Kork Head.

## Spunk

Spunk was a gold elf adventuring fighter and mage, noted for his eccentric bond to his hand puppet Grizzard, who operated around Ravens Bluff and the Vast region of North Faerûn in the 14 century DR.

Description
He had a rather handsome figure, replete with wavy brown hair and soft green eyes. He stood  and weighed .

Personality
Spunk had a rather neutral disposition. He was generally polite and soft spoken, friendly to those he adventured with, and giving off an air of morality and trustworthiness. However, he had very clownish, chaotic neutral tendencies. He was fun-loving, especially if that fun was had at the expense of humans, getting his jollies over the reactions generated by his eccentric puppeteering.

Abilities
By the age of 326, Spunk was skilled in the use of daggers, darts, knives, short swords, long swords, long bows, and staves. Out of all of these, he most favored bows and short swords. He was also quite proficient in the art of puppetry and ventriloquism, meaning he could make a puppet speak without visibly moving his lips.

In terms of magic, from his spellbook he kept on hand the spells cantrip, charm person, color spray, confusion, dimension door, flaming sphere, fireball, haste, knock, magic missile, non-detection, shocking grasp, sleep, stinking cloud, and web.

Possessions
Spunk's favorite attire at the age of 326 was a well-worn set of leather armor. Besides his spellbook he carried a long bow, a +1 short sword, and a pair of bracers of defense. His most prized possession was Grizzard, a wizard hand puppet consisting of a stared robe, pointed hat, and long white beard. He also carried an ensemble of miniature items for the puppet, including a backpack, glasses, pipe, spellbook, and tiny wand.

Activities
Throughout his time as an adventurer Spunk had a particular mischievous game that he loved to play on parties of mostly human adventurers. He would introduce himself to such adventuring companies as a skilled fighter looking for work, then if welcomed in would protest that he only travels in the company of his best friend "Grizzard", who he'd describe as a most wise and mysterious wizard that would be a valued member to their company. Once the adventurers seemed willing to his arrangement, he would stick his right hand into a knapsack and produce his hand puppet, introducing it with the full title, "Grizzard The Great and Wonderous, High Purveyor of Prestidigitation and Prince of Preponderance." If the adventurers seemed skeptical of Grizzard, Spunk would then demonstrate his power by having him cast a spell – in actuality casting the spell himself, but speaking verbal components through the puppet and having it perform the somatic gestures.

Whenever he performed ventriloquism with the puppet, Spunk acted earnestly towards it, showing Grizzard the utmost respect. If someone were to ever point out that Grizzard was merely a puppet, Spunk would feign distress and put on a melancholic, sulking act until offered an apology.

Grizzard was performed with a gravely voice and a personality that was both naughty as it was caustic – Spunk reasoned that such a demeanor was befitting of a smart and high stationed wizard. Grizzard was one to bark orders and to refuse making an appearance unless called for by his full title.

However, Spunk would never allow these antics to pose any real danger to those he adventured with and he performed confidently in his duties as a fighter. His ultimate goal with these charades was to get whatever adventuring company he was traveling with to truly believe in Grizzard and accept him as a member of their team. Once this was accomplished, Spunk would wait for an opportunity to sneak away and leave the party, never offering his compatriots any parting words or form of explanation.

History
The early life of Spunk was a mystery. He himself would assert that he was orphaned as a child in Cormanthor, then discovered and adopted by a clan of elves near the Vale of Lost Voices. When he set out into the world as an adventurer, he did so to fulfill his personal quest of meeting his biological parents.

Appendix
Background
Spunk was credited as the creation of RPGA member Rick Reid.

## Spirit of the Neverwinter Woods

The Spirit of the Neverwinter Woods was a fey defender of Neverwinter Wood in the 14 century DR.

History
Sometime shortly before the, the fey inhabitants of Neverwinter Wood faced a crisis that left the Spirit of the Neverwinter Woods wounded. In attempts to defend the forest, a group of dryads and nymphs crafted a powerful weapon – a fey spear, to be given to a hero willing to defend them and the Spirit. Before the fey spear found a hero, the crisis was over, and the weapon was discarded and forgotten.

## Spider-spun caparison

The spider-spun caparison was an extraordinary spidersilk outfit found in the Demonweb Pits in the 15 century DR.

Description
The caparison was all-black except for a dark red waistwrap. It was durable, flexible, and almost weightless.

Powers
The spider-spun caparison was best suited to monks, who benefited in their mastery of unarmed combat and use of ki while wearing it.

History
This outfit was found during the Sschindylryn invasion of Eveningstar—when Ymris the yugoloth worked with Cormyrean forces to invade the Realm of Ruin and kill Lolth's servant Kishnaurac. Along the way he may have summoned a group of arcanaloth reinforcements—only to have their services be out-bid by Kishnaurac and turned against him. The spider-spun caparison may have been taken from these overly-opportunistic fiends.

## Spider eater

Spider eaters were large hornet-like creatures with huge wings akin to those on bats.

Description
These large flying beasts weighed around  and had a wingspan of . They resembled a mix of giant hornets, giant bats, and giant spiders with long dexterous and venomous stingers.

Behavior
Spider eaters were so-named due to their tendency to lay their eggs in the bodies of paralyzed arachnids, and their near-uncanny ability to shrug off webs. Bloodsilk spiders were a favorite prey of these creatures and they could cause the bloodsilk spiders to vacate their native forests and flee into nearby ruins instead for safety.

Combat
When engaging in combat, they would use their stingers to deliver a dose of paralyzing venom, then retreat to a safe distance and wait for the venom to take effect. They hated having to give up their prey, and ranged attacks with arrow, bolt or spell would quickly gain a reaction from the spider eaters in the form of a total assault.

Ecology
When laying eggs, the female spider eater would always choose a creature that was large or larger in size, paralyze it with her stinger, then insert the eggs into the body of the victim. The eggs would over the course of six weeks hatch and then devour their host, emerging into the world ready to take flight.

Relationships
Spider eaters were sometimes trained as mounts by various individuals, most notably tasloi, who would raise these beasts as mounts for their most powerful members.

Usage
Training a spider eater to bear a rider usually took six weeks of hard work, but resulted in a large, dangerous, and agile mount. Fighting from atop a spider eater however was harder than just riding it and letting it do all the fighting, something it was quite capable of doing. They were able to carry up to  as a light load,  as a medium load, and up to  as a heavy load.

Due to their usefulness as mounts and beasts of burden both, their eggs would often go for as much as 2,000 gp on the open market, while young went up to 3,000 gp apiece. Another 3,000 gp were usually required to then hire a professional to actually train the spider eater, unless one wanted to make the attempt oneself.

History
In the, the beholder cult in the Temple in the Sky kept several spider eaters in their stables.
